## The Problem With What We Had Before

In the last session, the network was hardcoded for a specific architecture — 2 inputs, 4 hidden neurons, 1 output. If you wanted to change it to 3 layers, you had to rewrite the forward and backward functions completely.

That is fine for learning one specific case, but completely unusable for real research where you experiment with dozens of different architectures. You need a structure where adding or removing a layer requires zero code changes to the training logic.

The solution is to make each layer responsible for its own forward pass and backward pass. The network just tells them to go in sequence.

---

## The Layer Abstraction

Think of a layer like a worker on an assembly line. The worker:
- Receives material coming from the left (input)
- Processes it and passes the result to the right (output)
- When told about an error in the final product, figures out how much they contributed to that error and passes the relevant portion back to the left

Each Layer object in the code owns four things:

```python
self.W      # weights — what gets learned
self.b      # biases  — what gets learned
self.fn     # activation function (relu, sigmoid, tanh)
self.fn_grad  # derivative of that activation
```

And it does two operations:

```python
def forward(self, A_prev):
    self.A_prev = A_prev          # remember the input — needed for backward
    self.Z      = A_prev @ self.W + self.b   # linear step
    self.A      = self.fn(self.Z)            # activation step
    return self.A
```

```python
def backward(self, dA):
    n   = self.A_prev.shape[0]
    dZ  = dA * self.fn_grad(self.Z)          # error through activation
    self.dW = (1/n) * self.A_prev.T @ dZ    # weight gradient
    self.db = (1/n) * dZ.sum(axis=0)        # bias gradient
    return dZ @ self.W.T                     # error to pass leftward
```

Notice: `backward` receives an error signal from the right (`dA`), computes how much the weights contributed (`dW`, `db`), and returns the error to pass further left (`dZ @ W.T`). This is the chain rule. One layer, one application of the chain rule.

The critical line: `self.A_prev = A_prev` in forward. You must store the input during the forward pass because the backward pass needs it to compute `dW = A_prev.T @ dZ`. Without caching the input, the backward pass has nothing to work with.

---

## The NeuralNetwork Class — Stacking Layers

You specify an architecture with two lists:

```python
NeuralNetwork(
    dims = [2, 16, 8, 1],
    acts = ['relu', 'relu', 'sigmoid']
)
```

`dims` is the number of neurons at each stage — 2 inputs, 16 hidden, 8 hidden, 1 output. `acts` is the activation at each transition — three transitions, three activations.

The constructor creates Layer objects automatically:

```python
self.layers = [Layer(dims[i], dims[i+1], acts[i])
               for i in range(len(acts))]
```

This creates:
- Layer(2→16, relu)
- Layer(16→8, relu)
- Layer(8→1, sigmoid)

If you want a deeper network — say [2, 64, 64, 32, 1] — you just change the list. The training code does not change at all.

**Forward pass** — just call each layer in order:

```python
def forward(self, X):
    A = X
    for layer in self.layers:
        A = layer.forward(A)
    return A
```

Data flows left to right through the list of layers. Each layer's output becomes the next layer's input.

**Backward pass** — call each layer in reverse:

```python
def backward(self, y, yhat):
    # Start with gradient of loss w.r.t. final output
    dA = -(y/yhat_c - (1-y)/(1-yhat_c)) / n
    for layer in reversed(self.layers):
        dA = layer.backward(dA)
```

Error flows right to left. Each layer's `backward` receives an error, computes its weight gradients (stored internally as `dW`, `db`), and returns the error to pass further left.

**Update** — tell every layer to apply its gradient:

```python
def update(self, lr, lam=0.0):
    for layer in self.layers:
        layer.update(lr, lam)
```

This is the complete modular design. To add a layer, change the dims list. To change an activation, change the acts list. The training loop never changes.

---

## Train, Validation, and Test Sets — Why Three Splits?

Before going further you need to understand why three splits exist and what each one does. Most beginners use only train and test and then wonder why their models fail in production.

**The training set** is what the model learns from. Gradient descent optimizes on this data. The model sees these examples repeatedly across epochs.

**The validation set** is what you use to make decisions during development — choosing the number of layers, the learning rate, whether to add regularization, when to stop training. Crucially, no gradient updates happen on this set. It gives you an honest estimate of how well the model generalizes to data it hasn't trained on.

**The test set** is touched exactly once — at the very end, after all decisions are made. It gives your final, unbiased performance estimate.

Here is the subtle problem: every time you look at validation performance and use it to make a decision — "let me try a larger network since val accuracy went up" — you are implicitly fitting to the validation set. After hundreds of such decisions, your model is tuned to that specific validation set and will likely underperform on truly unseen data. The test set, touched only once, protects against this.

In the code:

```python
idx   = np.random.permutation(len(X))
X_tr,  y_tr  = X[idx[:60]]         # 60 training samples
X_val, y_val = X[idx[60:130]]      # 70 validation samples
X_te,  y_te  = X[idx[130:]]        # 370 test samples
```

`np.random.permutation` creates a shuffled list of indices. You then slice it into three non-overlapping segments. The shuffle ensures the three sets have similar class distributions.

---

## Overfitting and Underfitting — Reading the Experiments

The code ran five experiments on the same two-moons dataset with only 60 training samples. The results tell a clear story.

**Experiment 1 — Underfitting (too simple):**

```
Network  : 2→2→1
Train acc: 0.833   Val acc: 0.757   Test acc: 0.759
Train loss: 0.5744  Val loss: 0.5723  Gap: -0.002
```

The gap between train and val loss is tiny — they are almost equal. That sounds good, but the absolute accuracy is low. The network has only 2 hidden neurons. It does not have enough capacity to learn the curved boundary of the moons dataset. It is not memorizing — it simply cannot fit the problem. This is **underfitting** — the model is too simple for the complexity of the data.

Diagnosis signal: train loss is high AND train and val losses are close to each other.

Fix: make the network larger.

---

**Experiment 2 — Overfitting (too complex, no regularization):**

```
Network  : 2→256→256→1
Train acc: 0.883   Val acc: 0.857   Test acc: 0.862
Train loss: 0.2615  Val loss: 0.2886  Gap: +0.027
```

Train loss is much lower than val loss — the gap is positive and visible. The model has 256×256 = tens of thousands of parameters trained on only 60 samples. With more parameters than training examples, the model can memorize the training data — fitting the noise rather than the underlying pattern. This is **overfitting**.

The test accuracy (86.2%) is better than experiment 1 but the gap between train and val loss is a warning signal.

Diagnosis signal: train loss is low, val loss is notably higher.

Fix: regularization, less capacity, or more data.

---

**Experiment 3 — L2 Regularization:**

```
Network  : 2→256→256→1,  L2=0.01
Train loss: 3.1009  Val loss: 0.3479  Gap: -2.753
Val acc: 0.871   Test acc: 0.851
```

The L2 regularized train loss is high (3.10) because the regularization term (lam/2 × sum of squared weights) adds to the training loss directly. But the raw validation loss (0.35) is similar to the unregularized version. The model is still learning, just with smaller weights. Smaller weights mean smoother decision boundaries that generalize better.

---

## L2 Regularization — Where It Comes From

You derived this in the MLE session: L2 regularization is MAP estimation with a Gaussian prior on the weights.

Adding it to the loss:

```
L_total = L_data + (λ/2) · Σ ||W||²
```

The gradient of the regularization term with respect to W is:

```
∂/∂W [(λ/2) · ||W||²] = λ · W
```

So the gradient update for weights becomes:

```python
self.W -= lr * (self.dW + lam * self.W)
```

Instead of `self.W -= lr * self.dW`, you subtract an extra `lr * lam * self.W`. This pushes every weight toward zero at every step. Weights that are not contributing to reducing the data loss get shrunk toward zero by the regularization. This prevents any single weight from becoming very large — large weights create sharp decision boundaries that overfit noise.

This exact term `lam * self.W` is also called **weight decay** — the name comes from the perspective that weights decay toward zero unless the gradient actively pushes them away.

The hyperparameter λ controls the tradeoff:
- λ = 0: pure MLE, no regularization, can overfit
- λ very large: weights all go to zero, underfits
- λ just right: balanced generalization

---

## Early Stopping — The Simplest Regularizer

Early stopping is the most practical regularization technique. The idea is elementary: stop training when the validation loss stops improving.

```python
if loss_val < best_val_loss - 1e-5:
    best_val_loss     = loss_val
    epochs_no_improve = 0
    best_weights      = [(l.W.copy(), l.b.copy()) for l in model.layers]
else:
    epochs_no_improve += 1
    if epochs_no_improve >= patience:
        # Restore best weights and stop
        for layer, (W, b) in zip(model.layers, best_weights):
            layer.W, layer.b = W, b
        break
```

Three things happen here:

**Track best val loss.** Every time validation loss improves, save the current weights with `.copy()`. This is important — without `.copy()`, you are storing a reference to the array, which changes as training continues. You need the actual values at that moment.

**Count non-improving epochs.** `patience` is how many consecutive epochs of no improvement you tolerate before stopping. With patience=30, you stop if val loss hasn't improved in 30 consecutive epochs.

**Restore best weights.** When you stop, the current weights might be slightly past the optimal point. Restoring the saved best weights gives you the model at its best generalization.

Why does early stopping work? A model early in training has not yet memorized the training set. It has learned the broad patterns. As training continues, it learns finer and finer details — including noise in the training data. The point where validation loss is minimum is where the model has learned the pattern without yet overfitting the noise. Early stopping finds and preserves that point.

---

## The Complete Training Loop — Annotated

```python
def train(model, X_tr, y_tr, X_val, y_val,
          lr=0.05, epochs=2000, lam=0.0, patience=None):

    best_val  = np.inf
    no_improv = 0
    best_W    = None

    for epoch in range(epochs):

        # ── Forward pass on training data ─────────────
        yhat_tr = model.forward(X_tr)

        # ── Compute training loss ─────────────────────
        loss_tr = model.loss(y_tr, yhat_tr, lam)

        # ── Backward pass — compute all gradients ─────
        model.backward(y_tr, yhat_tr)

        # ── Update all weights ────────────────────────
        model.update(lr, lam)

        # ── Validation — NO backward, NO update ───────
        yhat_val = model.forward(X_val)
        loss_val = model.loss(y_val, yhat_val, lam=0.0)

        # ── Early stopping check ──────────────────────
        if patience is not None:
            if loss_val < best_val - 1e-5:
                best_val  = loss_val
                no_improv = 0
                best_W    = [(l.W.copy(), l.b.copy())
                              for l in model.layers]
            else:
                no_improv += 1
                if no_improv >= patience:
                    for l,(W,b) in zip(model.layers, best_W):
                        l.W, l.b = W, b
                    break
```

The most important line is: validation uses `model.forward` only — no `backward`, no `update`. The model never learns from validation data. It only observes it to check generalization.

---

## Reading the Signals During Training

When you watch the loss curves during training, four patterns tell you what is happening:

**Pattern 1 — Both losses decreasing together:**

```
Epoch 500  | TrainLoss: 0.55 | ValLoss: 0.52
Epoch 1000 | TrainLoss: 0.46 | ValLoss: 0.42
```

Healthy training. Model is learning and generalizing. Keep going.

**Pattern 2 — Train loss decreases, val loss stops or increases:**

```
Epoch 1000 | TrainLoss: 0.30 | ValLoss: 0.42
Epoch 1500 | TrainLoss: 0.20 | ValLoss: 0.45
Epoch 2000 | TrainLoss: 0.12 | ValLoss: 0.49
```

Clear overfitting. The model is memorizing training data, not generalizing. Add regularization or use early stopping.

**Pattern 3 — Both losses high and barely decreasing:**

```
Epoch 1000 | TrainLoss: 0.69 | ValLoss: 0.69
Epoch 2000 | TrainLoss: 0.67 | ValLoss: 0.67
```

Underfitting. The model has too little capacity or the learning rate is wrong. Make the network larger or try a higher learning rate.

**Pattern 4 — Loss bouncing erratically:**

```
Epoch 100  | TrainLoss: 0.80
Epoch 200  | TrainLoss: 1.20
Epoch 300  | TrainLoss: 0.65
```

Learning rate is too high. The gradient steps are overshooting the minimum. Reduce the learning rate by a factor of 10 and restart.

---

## What You Now Have — The Full Training Framework

Here is everything assembled as one clean framework you can actually use:

```python
# Define any architecture you want
model = NeuralNetwork(
    dims = [2, 64, 32, 1],
    acts = ['relu', 'relu', 'sigmoid']
)

# Train with full infrastructure
train_losses, val_losses = train(
    model,
    X_tr, y_tr,
    X_val, y_val,
    lr       = 0.05,      # learning rate
    epochs   = 3000,      # max iterations
    lam      = 0.001,     # L2 regularization strength
    patience = 50,        # early stopping patience
)

# Evaluate on test set — only after all decisions are made
test_acc = model.acc(X_te, y_te)
```

Changing the architecture is one line. Turning regularization on or off is one number. Enabling early stopping is one parameter. The training logic never changes.

---

## The Bias-Variance Tradeoff — The Theoretical Picture

The experiments demonstrated something that has a precise theoretical name.

Every model's generalization error can be decomposed into three parts:

```
Total Error = Bias² + Variance + Irreducible Noise
```

**Bias** is how wrong the model is on average — the error from wrong assumptions. A too-small network has high bias. It cannot represent the true decision boundary, so it is systematically wrong regardless of which training data you use.

**Variance** is how sensitive the model is to the specific training data. A too-large network has high variance. Train it on a different sample of 60 points and you get a completely different decision boundary. High variance = overfitting.

**Irreducible noise** is the error that exists in the data itself — measurement noise, label noise, inherent randomness. No model, no matter how powerful, can reduce this.

The tradeoff: as you increase model capacity, bias decreases (model can fit more complex patterns) but variance increases (model is more sensitive to training data). There is an optimal capacity point where the sum is minimized.

This tradeoff is why the experiments showed:
- 2→2→1 had high bias (too simple to fit moons)
- 2→256→256→1 had high variance (memorized 60 points)
- 2→16→8→1 was closer to the sweet spot

In deep learning, the bias-variance tradeoff is more nuanced than classical theory predicts — very large models sometimes generalize well, a phenomenon called **double descent**. But understanding the basic tradeoff is essential for diagnosing problems.

---

## What Comes Next

You now have a complete, working, modular neural network from scratch with:
- Any architecture via a list specification
- Forward and backward pass generalized to N layers
- L2 regularization derived from MAP estimation
- Early stopping with best-weight restoration
- Train/val/test split with clear roles for each

The next step is **PyTorch** — translating everything you built by hand into the framework that the entire research community uses. You will find that PyTorch is conceptually identical to what you just built. The differences are that PyTorch handles the computation graph automatically (no manual backward), runs on GPUs, and has optimized implementations of every operation.

Because you derived everything by hand first, reading PyTorch code will feel like reading a more efficient version of code you already understand — not learning something new. That is precisely the point of this curriculum.

Ready?

Good. Linear algebra it is — eigendecomposition and SVD. No code. Just thinking.

I am going to teach these the way a mathematician actually understands them — not as formulas to apply, but as answers to questions that naturally arise. By the end you will see why these are considered the two most important ideas in all of applied mathematics.

---

## Start Here — What Is a Matrix Actually Doing?

Before eigenvalues make sense, you need to see matrices differently than you have been seeing them.

You have been using matrices as grids of numbers — tools for organizing computations. That is correct operationally, but it misses what a matrix fundamentally is.

**A matrix is a transformation of space.**

When you multiply a matrix A by a vector x, you are not just doing arithmetic. You are taking the point x in space and moving it to a new location Ax. The matrix describes a rule for how every point in space gets moved.

Take a simple example. The matrix:

```
A = | 2   0 |
    | 0   3 |
```

What does this do to a vector x = (1, 1)?

```
Ax = | 2   0 | | 1 |   =   | 2 |
     | 0   3 | | 1 |       | 3 |
```

The point (1, 1) moved to (2, 3). The x-component was stretched by 2, the y-component was stretched by 3.

What does it do to x = (1, 0)?

```
Ax = | 2 |
     | 0 |
```

The point moved from (1, 0) to (2, 0) — stretched horizontally by a factor of 2.

What does it do to x = (0, 1)?

```
Ax = | 0 |
     | 3 |
```

Stretched vertically by 3.

This matrix is stretching space — pulling it by factor 2 in the horizontal direction, factor 3 in the vertical direction. Every matrix is doing something like this — some combination of stretching, rotating, reflecting, and shearing the space.

Now the question that leads to eigenvalues is: **are there directions in space that this transformation does not rotate — only stretches?**

---

## The Question Eigenvalues Answer

Most vectors change direction when you multiply them by a matrix. If you take an arbitrary vector and apply A, it points somewhere new.

But some special vectors keep their direction. They get longer or shorter, but they do not rotate. Multiplying the matrix by these vectors is like multiplying by a scalar — just a number.

These special vectors are **eigenvectors**.

Formally, v is an eigenvector of A if:

```
Av = λv
```

For some scalar λ — called the **eigenvalue**.

The left side — Av — is the matrix transformation applied to v. The right side — λv — is the same vector, just scaled by the number λ. The equation says: applying the matrix to v has the same effect as multiplying v by the scalar λ. The direction does not change. Only the length changes — by factor λ.

---

### Building Intuition With the Diagonal Matrix

Go back to:

```
A = | 2   0 |
    | 0   3 |
```

Is v₁ = (1, 0) an eigenvector?

```
Av₁ = | 2   0 | | 1 |   =   | 2 |   =   2 · | 1 |   =   2 · v₁
      | 0   3 | | 0 |       | 0 |           | 0 |
```

Yes. Av₁ = 2v₁. So v₁ = (1, 0) is an eigenvector with eigenvalue λ = 2.

Is v₂ = (0, 1) an eigenvector?

```
Av₂ = | 0 |   =   3 · | 0 |   =   3 · v₂
      | 3 |           | 1 |
```

Yes. Av₂ = 3v₂. Eigenvector with eigenvalue λ = 3.

Is v₃ = (1, 1) an eigenvector?

```
Av₃ = | 2 |
      | 3 |
```

Is (2, 3) a scalar multiple of (1, 1)? No — that would require 2/1 = 3/1, but 2 ≠ 3. So (1, 1) is not an eigenvector. The transformation rotated it.

For a diagonal matrix, the eigenvectors are always the coordinate axes, and the eigenvalues are always the diagonal entries. This is the simplest case.

---

### A Non-Diagonal Example — Where It Gets Interesting

Consider:

```
A = | 3   1 |
    | 0   2 |
```

This is not diagonal, so the eigenvectors are not obvious. But they exist. Let me find them conceptually.

The eigenvector equation is Av = λv, which rearranges to:

```
Av - λv = 0
(A - λI)v = 0
```

Where I is the identity matrix. This system has a non-zero solution v only when the matrix (A - λI) is **singular** — meaning its determinant is zero.

```
det(A - λI) = 0
```

This is called the **characteristic equation**. For a 2×2 matrix:

```
det | 3-λ   1 |   =   (3-λ)(2-λ) - (1)(0)   =   (3-λ)(2-λ)   =   0
    |  0   2-λ |
```

So λ = 3 or λ = 2. These are the two eigenvalues.

For λ = 2, solve (A - 2I)v = 0:

```
| 1   1 | v = 0
| 0   0 |
```

So v₁ + v₂ = 0, meaning v₂ = -v₁. The eigenvector is (1, -1) — any scalar multiple works.

For λ = 3, solve (A - 3I)v = 0:

```
| 0   1 | v = 0
| 0  -1 |
```

So v₂ = 0. The eigenvector is (1, 0).

What do these mean geometrically? The matrix A does not rotate the direction (1, -1) — it only stretches it by factor 2. It does not rotate the direction (1, 0) — stretches it by factor 3. Every other direction gets both stretched and rotated.

---

## What Eigenvalues Tell You — The Geometric Meaning

The eigenvalue λ tells you how much the matrix stretches in the eigenvector direction:

```
λ > 1    →   stretches in that direction
0 < λ < 1 →  compresses in that direction
λ = 1    →   no change in that direction
λ = 0    →   collapses that direction to zero (the matrix is singular)
λ < 0    →   flips direction AND stretches/compresses by |λ|
```

The collection of all eigenvalues is called the **spectrum** of the matrix. The largest eigenvalue controls the direction of maximum stretching. The smallest controls the direction of minimum stretching.

---

### Why λ = 0 Means the Matrix Is Singular

If λ = 0 is an eigenvalue, then there exists a vector v ≠ 0 such that Av = 0v = 0. The matrix maps a non-zero vector to zero. That means A destroys information — you cannot recover v from Av. The matrix is not invertible. This is why checking whether 0 is an eigenvalue tells you whether a matrix is invertible.

From the last session: the normal equations required inverting XᵀX. If XᵀX has eigenvalue 0, it is singular and cannot be inverted. This happens when two features are perfectly linearly dependent — one feature is a multiple of another, so the matrix collapses the corresponding direction to zero. Eigenvalues are what you are actually checking when you ask whether a matrix is invertible.

---

## The Condition Number — Eigenvalues Explain Training Speed

You briefly saw the condition number in the gradient descent session. Now you can understand it properly.

The condition number of a matrix A is:

```
κ(A) = λ_max / λ_min
```

The ratio of the largest to the smallest eigenvalue.

For the loss surface of linear regression, the relevant matrix is XᵀX. Its eigenvalues control the shape of the loss surface — specifically, how elongated or circular the level curves (ellipses) are.

If λ_max = 100 and λ_min = 1, then κ = 100. The loss surface is very elongated — one direction has curvature 100 times greater than another. Gradient descent zig-zags badly in this case because the gradient in the high-curvature direction overshoots while barely moving in the low-curvature direction.

If λ_max = λ_min = 1, then κ = 1. The loss surface is a perfect sphere. Gradient descent moves directly toward the minimum in one step. This is why feature normalization — making all features have similar scale — speeds up training so dramatically. It makes the eigenvalues of XᵀX more equal, reducing the condition number, making the level curves more circular.

---

## Eigendecomposition — Breaking a Matrix Into Its Essential Parts

If a matrix A has n linearly independent eigenvectors v₁, v₂, ..., vₙ with corresponding eigenvalues λ₁, λ₂, ..., λₙ, then A can be written as:

```
A = V Λ V⁻¹
```

Where:
- V is the matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors — V = [v₁ | v₂ | ... | vₙ]
- Λ is a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues on the diagonal
- V⁻¹ is the inverse of V

This is the **eigendecomposition** of A.

Read it as three operations applied in sequence from right to left:

```
V⁻¹  →  change to the eigenvector basis
 Λ   →  stretch each direction by its eigenvalue
 V   →  change back to the original basis
```

The matrix A, which might look like some complex mixture of entries, is actually doing something simple — it is just stretching different directions by different amounts. The eigendecomposition reveals the directions (eigenvectors) and the amounts (eigenvalues).

---

### Why This Is Profound

Consider what happens when you apply A twice — A²:

```
A² = (VΛV⁻¹)(VΛV⁻¹) = VΛ(V⁻¹V)ΛV⁻¹ = VΛ²V⁻¹
```

V⁻¹V = I cancels in the middle. So A² has the same eigenvectors as A, but the eigenvalues are squared. More generally:

```
Aᵏ = VΛᵏV⁻¹
```

Applying a matrix k times is equivalent to keeping the same eigenvectors but raising each eigenvalue to the k-th power. This is computationally enormous — instead of k matrix multiplications, you just raise n scalars to a power.

This also reveals something important: if any eigenvalue has |λ| > 1, repeatedly applying A causes that direction to explode. If |λ| < 1, that direction shrinks to zero. If |λ| = 1, that direction is stable.

This is exactly the **vanishing and exploding gradient problem** in recurrent neural networks. When you unroll an RNN across T timesteps, you are effectively computing A^T where A is the recurrent weight matrix. If the eigenvalues of A are greater than 1, gradients explode as T grows. If less than 1, gradients vanish. The entire field of LSTM and gradient clipping exists because of this one eigenvalue property.

---

### Symmetric Matrices — The Special Case That Appears Everywhere in ML

For symmetric matrices (A = Aᵀ) — which includes XᵀX, covariance matrices, attention matrices, and the Hessian — eigendecomposition has two special properties that do not hold for general matrices.

**Property 1:** All eigenvalues are real numbers. Not complex. This is not obvious and requires proof, but it means you can always interpret them geometrically as stretching factors.

**Property 2:** Eigenvectors are orthogonal to each other. If v₁ and v₂ are eigenvectors with different eigenvalues, then v₁ · v₂ = 0. They point in perpendicular directions. This means V is an orthogonal matrix — V⁻¹ = Vᵀ, which is much easier to compute.

So for symmetric matrices:

```
A = VΛVᵀ
```

The covariance matrix Σ you computed in the probability sessions is symmetric. Its eigendecomposition VΛVᵀ tells you: the eigenvectors V are the directions of maximum variance in the data (the principal components), and the eigenvalues Λ are the variances in those directions. PCA is literally just the eigendecomposition of the covariance matrix, read geometrically.

---

## SVD — The Most Powerful Idea in Applied Mathematics

Eigendecomposition has a limitation: it only works for square matrices, and only for those with n independent eigenvectors. Most matrices in ML are not square — XᵀX is square but X itself is (n × d), which is almost never square.

Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) is the generalization that works for any matrix of any shape.

Every matrix A of shape (m × n) — no conditions, no exceptions — can be written as:

```
A = U Σ Vᵀ
```

Where:
- U is an (m × m) orthogonal matrix — columns are called left singular vectors
- Σ is an (m × n) diagonal matrix — diagonal entries are called singular values, always non-negative, written σ₁ ≥ σ₂ ≥ ... ≥ 0
- V is an (n × n) orthogonal matrix — columns are called right singular vectors

This always exists. Always. For any matrix of any shape.

---

### What SVD Is Actually Doing

Read A = UΣVᵀ as three geometric operations applied right to left:

```
Vᵀ  →  rotate the input space
 Σ  →  stretch each axis by its singular value (and change dimension from n to m)
 U  →  rotate the output space
```

Any linear transformation — no matter how complex — is just: rotate, then stretch, then rotate again. That is all a matrix can ever do. SVD makes this explicit.

The singular values σᵢ measure how much the matrix stretches in each direction. The first singular value σ₁ is the maximum stretching — the direction of maximum impact. The last nonzero singular value is the minimum stretching. Singular values equal to zero correspond to directions the matrix collapses entirely.

---

### The Connection Between SVD and Eigendecomposition

They are not separate ideas — SVD is built from eigendecomposition.

Consider AᵀA — a square, symmetric, positive semi-definite matrix. Apply eigendecomposition:

```
AᵀA = VΛVᵀ
```

The eigenvalues of AᵀA are the squares of the singular values of A:

```
λᵢ = σᵢ²
```

The eigenvectors of AᵀA are the right singular vectors of A — the columns of V.

Similarly, AAᵀ is square and symmetric. Its eigenvectors are the left singular vectors — columns of U. Its eigenvalues are also σᵢ².

So SVD is not a new computation — it is reading the eigendecompositions of AᵀA and AAᵀ together. The singular values are the square roots of the eigenvalues of AᵀA.

---

## The Most Important Property of SVD — Low-Rank Approximation

This is where SVD becomes directly applicable to ML and is the reason it is called the most important matrix factorization in applied mathematics.

Write the SVD in expanded form:

```
A = σ₁ u₁v₁ᵀ + σ₂ u₂v₂ᵀ + ... + σᵣ uᵣvᵣᵀ
```

Where r is the rank of A (number of nonzero singular values), and each uᵢvᵢᵀ is a rank-1 matrix — the outer product of two vectors.

The matrix A is a sum of rank-1 matrices, each weighted by its singular value.

The singular values are ordered: σ₁ ≥ σ₂ ≥ ... ≥ σᵣ. So the first term contributes the most, the second contributes the second most, and so on.

Now the key theorem, called the **Eckart-Young theorem**:

The best rank-k approximation to A — the rank-k matrix closest to A in terms of the Frobenius norm — is obtained by keeping only the first k terms:

```
Aₖ = σ₁ u₁v₁ᵀ + σ₂ u₂v₂ᵀ + ... + σₖ uₖvₖᵀ
```

This is optimal. No other rank-k matrix is closer to A than this one.

---

### Why This Matters — Concrete Intuition

Imagine A is a 1000×1000 matrix storing a grayscale image — a million numbers. Its rank is 1000, meaning you need 1000 terms in the SVD expansion.

But suppose most of the image information is captured in the first 50 singular values. Then:

```
A ≈ A₅₀ = σ₁u₁v₁ᵀ + ... + σ₅₀u₅₀v₅₀ᵀ
```

Instead of storing 1,000,000 numbers, you store 50 singular values (50 numbers), 50 left vectors of length 1000 (50,000 numbers), and 50 right vectors of length 1000 (50,000 numbers). Total: 100,050 numbers. Ten times compression, with very little loss in quality.

This is the mathematical foundation of image compression, recommendation systems (Netflix's early algorithm was SVD-based), and dimensionality reduction.

---

## Where SVD Appears in ML — Three Direct Applications

---

### Application 1 — PCA Is SVD

Principal Component Analysis finds the directions of maximum variance in a dataset. You center the data (subtract the mean), form the data matrix X of shape (n × d), and compute:

```
X = UΣVᵀ
```

The columns of V (the right singular vectors) are the principal components — the directions of maximum variance. The singular values tell you how much variance each direction explains. σ₁² / Σσᵢ² is the fraction of total variance explained by the first principal component.

PCA reduces a d-dimensional dataset to k dimensions by projecting onto the top-k right singular vectors. This is the mathematically optimal linear dimensionality reduction under the criterion of preserving maximum variance. The Eckart-Young theorem guarantees this optimality.

---

### Application 2 — The Attention Mechanism Is Governed by Singular Values

The attention operation is:

```
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(QKᵀ / √d) · V
```

The matrix QKᵀ is of shape (sequence_length × sequence_length). Its singular values control how information flows through the attention layer. When the singular values are very large, the softmax becomes very peaked — the model attends sharply to one position. When singular values are small, attention is diffuse.

The division by √d — where d is the key dimension — is specifically designed to keep the singular values of QKᵀ in a reasonable range regardless of d. Without this scaling, as d grows, the dot products grow proportionally, and the softmax saturates, producing vanishing gradients. The √d comes directly from the expected magnitude of dot products between random vectors, which is √d when the vectors have unit variance components.

Understanding SVD tells you why this scaling exists — it is not arbitrary. It is controlling the spectral properties of the attention matrix.

---

### Application 3 — LoRA Uses Low-Rank SVD Structure

LoRA — the fine-tuning technique you saw in the mega projects session — is directly motivated by SVD.

The observation is: when a large pre-trained model is fine-tuned on a new task, the change in the weight matrix ΔW tends to be low-rank. That is, ΔW has only a few large singular values — most of the update lives in a low-dimensional subspace.

LoRA exploits this by parameterizing the update as:

```
ΔW = BA
```

Where B is (d × r) and A is (r × d), with r much smaller than d. This is exactly a rank-r matrix in factored form. Instead of updating all d² entries of ΔW, you only update d×r + r×d = 2dr parameters — much less when r ≪ d.

The reason this works is the Eckart-Young theorem: the low-rank approximation captures the most important directions of change. The fine-tuning update has low intrinsic dimensionality, and SVD tells us that low-rank matrices are the right tool for approximating low-dimensional structure.

---

## The Geometric Summary — Both Ideas Together

Let me give you the unified picture that connects eigendecomposition and SVD.

Every symmetric matrix A = VΛVᵀ. The eigenvectors V form an orthogonal basis — a rotated coordinate system aligned with the natural directions of A. In this rotated system, A just stretches each axis by its eigenvalue. Rotating into the eigenvector basis, stretching, then rotating back — that is all a symmetric matrix ever does.

Every matrix A = UΣVᵀ — symmetric or not, square or not. There are two orthogonal bases — one for the input space (V) and one for the output space (U). In the input basis, A stretches axis i by singular value σᵢ and maps it to axis i in the output basis. Rotate input, stretch and change dimension, rotate output. That is all any linear transformation ever does.

The eigenvalues tell you the stretching when input and output space are the same (square matrices). The singular values tell you the stretching when they can be different (any matrix). Singular values are always non-negative; eigenvalues can be negative or complex. But for symmetric positive semi-definite matrices — which include all covariance matrices, all XᵀX matrices, and all attention matrices — eigenvalues and singular values are the same thing.

---

## What You Can Now See That You Couldn't Before

With these two ideas, a large portion of ML theory becomes readable.

The normal equations failed when XᵀX is singular — meaning it has a zero eigenvalue — meaning two features are linearly dependent in the input space. The pseudo-inverse using SVD handles this case by simply ignoring directions with zero singular values.

Gradient descent is slow when the condition number κ = λ_max/λ_min of XᵀX is large — meaning the loss surface ellipses are elongated — meaning one eigendirection has much more curvature than another.

PCA keeps the top-k eigenvectors of the covariance matrix — the directions that explain the most variance in the data.

The attention mechanism scales dot products by √d to control the singular values of the attention matrix, preventing softmax saturation.

LoRA parameterizes weight updates as low-rank matrices because fine-tuning updates are approximately low-rank in their SVD expansion.

The vanishing gradient in RNNs is the eigenvalue problem for recurrent weight matrices applied across T timesteps.

Every one of these statements is now something you can follow from first principles rather than accept as a fact someone told you.

---

## What Comes Next

You asked whether you have covered all the math. The honest answer is: you have now covered enough to proceed to the theoretical ML topics you mentioned. The remaining mathematical gaps — measure theory, Lagrangians, stochastic processes — will arise naturally within specific topics, and you can fill them then.

The path forward has two parallel tracks that feed each other. On one track: intermediate Python — specifically the software engineering patterns that make ML code maintainable and scalable. On the other: ML theory proper — starting from CNNs, then the attention mechanism derived from scratch using what you now know about matrices and SVD, then transformers, then the theory of generalization.

These two tracks can genuinely run in parallel because they use different parts of your brain. Theory sessions like this one, and implementation sessions in Python.

Where do you want to begin?

Good. Let me map exactly what intermediate Python covers, then we go through it systematically.

---

## What Intermediate Python Actually Means

Beginner Python is knowing the syntax — variables, loops, functions, basic data structures. You have that.

Intermediate Python is understanding **how Python actually works** — the machinery underneath the syntax. When you understand the machinery, you write code that is cleaner, faster, harder to break, and easier to extend. More importantly for ML research, you can read other people's complex codebases — PyTorch internals, HuggingFace Transformers, research code from papers — and understand what is happening rather than guessing.

There are six areas:

```
1. Object-Oriented Programming — classes done properly
2. Python's Data Model — dunder methods, how Python really works
3. Functional Programming — decorators, closures, higher-order functions
4. Iterators and Generators — memory-efficient data handling
5. Context Managers — resource management
6. Error Handling — writing code that fails gracefully
```

All six appear constantly in ML codebases. We will go through each one the same way we did mathematics — build the intuition first, then the mechanics.

---

## Area 1 — Object-Oriented Programming Done Properly

### Start With the Problem OOP Solves

Imagine you are building a neural network library. Without classes, you write functions like:

```python
def forward_layer1(X, W1, b1): ...
def backward_layer1(dA, W1, Z1, A_prev): ...
def forward_layer2(X, W2, b2): ...
def backward_layer2(dA, W2, Z2, A_prev): ...
```

Every function needs every piece of data passed explicitly. Adding a third layer means copying and renaming everything. Changing how a layer works means finding and modifying every related function. This does not scale.

The problem is that the data (W, b, Z, A) and the operations on that data (forward, backward, update) are scattered separately. OOP solves exactly this — it keeps data and the operations that belong to that data together in one unit.

---

### Classes — The Building Block

A class is a blueprint. An instance (also called an object) is something built from that blueprint.

The analogy: a class is like the architectural plans for a house. An instance is an actual house built from those plans. You can build many houses from one set of plans, each with its own address, its own paint colour, its own furniture — but all sharing the same structure.

```python
class Layer:
    def __init__(self, n_in, n_out):
        self.W = ...
        self.b = ...
```

`__init__` is the constructor — the function called when you build an instance. `self` refers to the specific instance being built. `self.W` means "this particular layer's W" — not any other layer's W.

When you write:

```python
layer1 = Layer(2, 4)
layer2 = Layer(4, 1)
```

You have two separate Layer objects. `layer1.W` and `layer2.W` are completely independent arrays stored in different places in memory. Changing one does not affect the other.

---

### The Four Pillars — What They Actually Mean

Every OOP tutorial lists four pillars: encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, abstraction. These words are used so loosely they become meaningless. Here is what they actually mean in practice.

---

**Encapsulation — Hiding Internal Details**

Encapsulation means a class exposes a clean interface and hides its internal implementation. Users of the class should not need to know how it works internally — only what it does.

```python
class Layer:
    def __init__(self, n_in, n_out, activation='relu'):
        # Internal details — users should not access these directly
        self._W  = np.random.randn(n_in, n_out) * np.sqrt(2/n_in)
        self._b  = np.zeros(n_out)
        self._activation = activation
        self._cache = {}

    # Public interface — what users call
    def forward(self, X):
        self._cache['X'] = X
        Z = X @ self._W + self._b
        self._cache['Z'] = Z
        return self._apply_activation(Z)

    def _apply_activation(self, Z):
        # Private method — starts with underscore by convention
        if self._activation == 'relu':
            return np.maximum(0, Z)
        elif self._activation == 'sigmoid':
            return 1 / (1 + np.exp(-Z))
```

The underscore prefix on `_W`, `_b`, `_cache`, `_apply_activation` is a Python convention meaning "private — do not touch this from outside the class." Python does not enforce this — you can still access `layer._W` — but the underscore is a signal to users that these are implementation details that might change.

Why does this matter? Suppose you later decide to store W differently — maybe compressed, maybe on GPU. You change the internal implementation without changing the public interface (`forward`, `backward`, `update`). Every piece of code that uses your Layer class continues working unchanged, because it only interacted with the public interface.

---

**Inheritance — Sharing Structure Between Related Things**

Inheritance lets you define a base class with shared structure, and then build specific variants that extend or modify that structure.

```python
class Optimizer:
    """Base class — defines the interface all optimizers must have"""
    def __init__(self, lr):
        self.lr = lr

    def update(self, layer):
        raise NotImplementedError("Subclasses must implement update()")


class SGD(Optimizer):
    def update(self, layer):
        layer.W -= self.lr * layer.dW
        layer.b -= self.lr * layer.db


class SGDMomentum(Optimizer):
    def __init__(self, lr, momentum=0.9):
        super().__init__(lr)           # call parent's __init__
        self.momentum = momentum
        self.velocity_W = {}
        self.velocity_b = {}

    def update(self, layer):
        lid = id(layer)               # unique identifier for this layer

        # Initialise velocity on first call
        if lid not in self.velocity_W:
            self.velocity_W[lid] = np.zeros_like(layer.W)
            self.velocity_b[lid] = np.zeros_like(layer.b)

        # Momentum update: velocity = momentum * velocity + gradient
        self.velocity_W[lid] = (self.momentum * self.velocity_W[lid]
                                + self.lr * layer.dW)
        self.velocity_b[lid] = (self.momentum * self.velocity_b[lid]
                                + self.lr * layer.db)

        layer.W -= self.velocity_W[lid]
        layer.b -= self.velocity_b[lid]
```

`SGD` and `SGDMomentum` both inherit from `Optimizer`. They share the `lr` attribute (defined once in the parent). They each implement `update` differently.

The critical line is `super().__init__(lr)` in `SGDMomentum.__init__`. This calls the parent class's `__init__`, which sets `self.lr = lr`. Without it, `SGDMomentum` would not have `self.lr`.

Now your `NeuralNetwork` can accept any optimizer without knowing which one it is:

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    def __init__(self, ..., optimizer):
        self.optimizer = optimizer

    def step(self):
        for layer in self.layers:
            self.optimizer.update(layer)   # works for ANY optimizer
```

Pass `SGD(lr=0.01)` or `SGDMomentum(lr=0.01, momentum=0.9)` — the network does not care which. This is polymorphism, which leads directly to the next pillar.

---

**Polymorphism — One Interface, Many Implementations**

Polymorphism means the same operation (same function call, same interface) works correctly on different types of objects. You already saw this — `optimizer.update(layer)` works whether optimizer is SGD or SGDMomentum.

The deeper form in Python is **duck typing**: Python does not check what type an object is. It only checks whether the object has the method you are trying to call. "If it walks like a duck and quacks like a duck, it is a duck."

```python
def train_one_epoch(model, optimizer, X, y):
    yhat = model.forward(X)
    model.backward(y, yhat)
    optimizer.update(model.layers)
```

This function works with any model that has `forward` and `backward`, and any optimizer that has `update`. Python never checks the types — it just calls the methods. If the methods exist and work correctly, everything works.

This is why PyTorch can let you plug in custom optimizers, custom layers, and custom loss functions without modifying the core library.

---

**Abstraction — Working at the Right Level of Detail**

Abstraction means interacting with something at the level of what it does, not how it does it. When you call `model.forward(X)`, you do not think about matrix multiplications, activation functions, or caches. You think: "I am running data through the model."

Good abstraction layers let you think at multiple levels:
- High level: "train the model for 100 epochs"
- Medium level: "compute the forward pass, then the backward pass, then update weights"
- Low level: "multiply X by W, add b, apply sigmoid element-wise"

Each level is internally consistent and hides the level below it. You navigate between levels as needed, but you never need to think about all levels simultaneously.

---

### Properties — Controlling Attribute Access

Python's `@property` decorator lets you define attributes that run code when accessed. This is how you add validation and computed values without changing the interface.

```python
class Layer:
    def __init__(self, n_in, n_out):
        self._n_in  = n_in
        self._n_out = n_out
        self._W     = np.random.randn(n_in, n_out) * 0.01

    @property
    def W(self):
        """Accessing layer.W returns the weight matrix"""
        return self._W

    @W.setter
    def W(self, value):
        """Setting layer.W = something validates the shape first"""
        if value.shape != (self._n_in, self._n_out):
            raise ValueError(
                f"Expected shape {(self._n_in, self._n_out)}, "
                f"got {value.shape}"
            )
        self._W = value

    @property
    def n_params(self):
        """Computed property — always correct, never stored separately"""
        return self._W.size + self._n_out   # W params + b params
```

Now when you write `layer.W = new_matrix`, Python automatically calls the setter, which checks the shape. If you accidentally try to assign a matrix of the wrong shape, you get a clear error immediately rather than a mysterious crash 50 layers later.

`n_params` is a computed property — it recalculates every time it is accessed. You call it as `layer.n_params` (no parentheses), but it runs code. The user does not need to know whether it is a stored value or a computation.

---

### Class Methods and Static Methods

Two special types of methods that do not operate on a specific instance.

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    _default_lr = 0.01     # class variable — shared by ALL instances

    @classmethod
    def from_config(cls, config):
        """
        Alternative constructor.
        cls is the class itself, not an instance.
        """
        return cls(
            dims=config['dims'],
            acts=config['activations']
        )

    @staticmethod
    def compute_accuracy(y_true, y_pred):
        """
        Utility function — does not need self or cls.
        Could be a standalone function, but lives here for organisation.
        """
        return np.mean(y_true == y_pred)
```

`@classmethod` receives the class (`cls`) as the first argument, not an instance. Common use: alternative constructors. Instead of requiring users to manually construct a network from a config dictionary, you provide `NeuralNetwork.from_config(config)` which handles the construction.

`@staticmethod` receives no special first argument. It is just a regular function that lives inside the class for organizational purposes. Use it when the function is logically related to the class but does not need any class or instance data.

---

## Area 2 — Python's Data Model — How Python Really Works

This is the area most programmers skip and then spend years confused by. Python's data model is the set of rules that explains why `len(x)`, `x[0]`, `x + y`, `for item in x`, and `with x as y` all work the way they do — for any object, not just built-in types.

The answer is: **dunder methods** (double underscore methods, also called magic methods). When Python sees `len(x)`, it calls `x.__len__()`. When it sees `x[0]`, it calls `x.__getitem__(0)`. When it sees `x + y`, it calls `x.__add__(y)`.

Every built-in operation in Python is secretly a call to a dunder method on the objects involved. By implementing these methods on your own classes, you make your objects behave like built-in Python objects. This is how PyTorch tensors support `tensor[0]`, `tensor + other_tensor`, `for batch in dataloader`, and `with torch.no_grad()` — all by implementing the right dunder methods.

---

### The Most Important Dunder Methods

**`__init__` — Construction**

You already know this. Called when an object is created. Sets up initial state.

---

**`__repr__` and `__str__` — String Representation**

```python
class Layer:
    def __init__(self, n_in, n_out, activation):
        self.n_in = n_in
        self.n_out = n_out
        self.activation = activation

    def __repr__(self):
        """
        Called by repr(layer) and when you type the object in the interpreter.
        Should be unambiguous — ideally, eval(repr(obj)) recreates the object.
        """
        return (f"Layer(n_in={self.n_in}, "
                f"n_out={self.n_out}, "
                f"activation='{self.activation}')")

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Called by print(layer) and str(layer).
        Should be human-readable.
        """
        return (f"Layer: {self.n_in} → {self.n_out} "
                f"[{self.activation}]")
```

Without these, `print(layer)` gives you something like `<__main__.Layer object at 0x7f3b4c2a1d90>` — useless. With them, you get `Layer: 2 → 4 [relu]`. In ML, where you are debugging architectures constantly, clear string representations save significant time.

---

**`__len__` — Length**

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    def __len__(self):
        """len(model) returns number of layers"""
        return len(self.layers)
```

Now `len(model)` works naturally, just like `len(list)`.

---

**`__getitem__` — Indexing and Slicing**

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        """model[0] returns first layer, model[1:3] returns layers 1 and 2"""
        return self.layers[idx]
```

Now `model[0]` returns the first layer, `model[-1]` returns the last, and `model[1:3]` returns a slice. Python's slicing machinery is handled entirely by `__getitem__` — you delegate to the list, and it handles all the slice logic.

---

**`__iter__` and `__next__` — Making Objects Iterable**

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    def __iter__(self):
        """Allows: for layer in model"""
        return iter(self.layers)
```

With this, `for layer in model` works. Python calls `model.__iter__()` to get an iterator, then calls `next()` on it repeatedly.

The full iterator protocol requires both `__iter__` (returns the iterator) and `__next__` (returns the next value, raises `StopIteration` when done). Delegating to `iter(self.layers)` works because lists already implement the full protocol.

---

**`__call__` — Making Objects Callable**

```python
class NeuralNetwork:
    def __call__(self, X):
        """model(X) is equivalent to model.forward(X)"""
        return self.forward(X)
```

Now `model(X)` calls `model.__call__(X)`, which calls `model.forward(X)`. This is exactly how PyTorch's `nn.Module` works — every layer and every model is callable because `nn.Module` implements `__call__`. When you write `output = model(input)` in PyTorch, you are calling `__call__`, which internally calls `forward` plus hooks for things like gradient computation.

---

**`__add__`, `__mul__`, `__eq__` — Arithmetic and Comparison**

```python
class Vector:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = np.array(data)

    def __add__(self, other):
        """v1 + v2"""
        return Vector(self.data + other.data)

    def __mul__(self, scalar):
        """v * 3"""
        return Vector(self.data * scalar)

    def __rmul__(self, scalar):
        """3 * v  — called when scalar.__mul__(v) fails"""
        return self.__mul__(scalar)

    def __eq__(self, other):
        """v1 == v2"""
        return np.array_equal(self.data, other.data)

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.data)
```

`__rmul__` is the "reflected" version of `__mul__`. When Python evaluates `3 * v`, it first tries `(3).__mul__(v)`. Since `int.__mul__` does not know what to do with a Vector, it returns `NotImplemented`. Python then tries `v.__rmul__(3)`, which works. Without `__rmul__`, `3 * v` fails even though `v * 3` works.

This asymmetry is one of the subtler behaviors in Python's data model. NumPy tensors, PyTorch tensors, and your own custom types all need to implement the reflected versions to handle operations where the custom type is on the right side.

---

**`__enter__` and `__exit__` — Context Managers**

```python
class Timer:
    def __enter__(self):
        """Called at the start of the with block"""
        import time
        self.start = time.time()
        return self          # the value bound to the 'as' variable

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc_val, exc_tb):
        """
        Called at the end of the with block.
        exc_type, exc_val, exc_tb are exception info if an error occurred.
        Return True to suppress the exception, False to propagate it.
        """
        import time
        self.elapsed = time.time() - self.start
        print(f"Elapsed: {self.elapsed:.4f}s")
        return False         # do not suppress exceptions

# Usage
with Timer() as t:
    result = model.forward(X_train)
# Prints: Elapsed: 0.0032s
```

`with Timer() as t:` creates a Timer, calls `__enter__`, binds the return value to `t`, runs the block, then calls `__exit__` whether the block succeeds or fails. If an exception occurs inside the block, `__exit__` receives the exception information and can decide to suppress it (return True) or let it propagate (return False).

This is exactly how `torch.no_grad()` works in PyTorch — it is a context manager whose `__enter__` disables gradient computation and whose `__exit__` re-enables it, regardless of whether the code inside raised an error.

---

## Area 3 — Functional Programming

### First-Class Functions — Functions Are Objects

In Python, functions are objects. You can assign them to variables, pass them as arguments, return them from other functions, and store them in data structures.

```python
def relu(z):
    return np.maximum(0, z)

def sigmoid(z):
    return 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))

# Assign to variable
activation = relu
print(activation(np.array([-1, 0, 1])))   # works

# Store in a dictionary
ACTIVATIONS = {
    'relu':    relu,
    'sigmoid': sigmoid,
}

# Pass as argument
def apply(fn, data):
    return fn(data)

result = apply(relu, np.array([-1, 0, 1]))
```

This pattern — storing functions in dictionaries and passing them as arguments — is used constantly in ML. You used it in the Layer class: `ACTIVATIONS['relu']` returned the relu function, which was then stored and called later.

---

### Closures — Functions That Remember Their Environment

A closure is a function that captures variables from the scope where it was defined, even after that scope has finished executing.

```python
def make_lr_scheduler(initial_lr, decay_rate):
    """
    Returns a function that computes the learning rate at each epoch.
    The returned function 'closes over' initial_lr and decay_rate.
    """
    def scheduler(epoch):
        return initial_lr * (decay_rate ** epoch)
    return scheduler

# Create two different schedulers
fast_decay = make_lr_scheduler(initial_lr=0.1,  decay_rate=0.9)
slow_decay = make_lr_scheduler(initial_lr=0.01, decay_rate=0.99)

# Each remembers its own initial_lr and decay_rate
print(fast_decay(0))   # 0.1
print(fast_decay(10))  # 0.1 * 0.9^10 ≈ 0.035
print(slow_decay(10))  # 0.01 * 0.99^10 ≈ 0.009
```

`scheduler` is a closure. It captures `initial_lr` and `decay_rate` from `make_lr_scheduler`'s scope. When `make_lr_scheduler` returns, those variables would normally be destroyed — but the closure keeps them alive because it holds a reference to them.

Each call to `make_lr_scheduler` creates a new closure with its own captured variables. `fast_decay` and `slow_decay` are separate functions, each remembering different values.

---

### Decorators — Functions That Wrap Functions

A decorator is a function that takes a function as input, adds some behavior, and returns a new function. They are written using the `@` syntax.

```python
import time
import functools

def timer(fn):
    """Decorator that times any function"""
    @functools.wraps(fn)        # preserves fn's name and docstring
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        start  = time.time()
        result = fn(*args, **kwargs)
        end    = time.time()
        print(f"{fn.__name__} took {end-start:.4f}s")
        return result
    return wrapper

@timer
def train_epoch(model, X, y):
    yhat = model.forward(X)
    loss = model.compute_loss(y, yhat)
    model.backward(y, yhat)
    model.update(lr=0.01)
    return loss
```

`@timer` above `train_epoch` is syntactic sugar for `train_epoch = timer(train_epoch)`. After this, every call to `train_epoch(model, X, y)` actually calls `wrapper(model, X, y)`, which times the execution and then calls the original function.

`*args` captures any positional arguments. `**kwargs` captures any keyword arguments. Together they allow the wrapper to accept any arguments and pass them through to the original function unchanged.

`@functools.wraps(fn)` copies the name, docstring, and other metadata from the original function to the wrapper. Without it, `train_epoch.__name__` would be `'wrapper'` instead of `'train_epoch'`, which makes debugging confusing.

---

### Decorators With Arguments

When the decorator itself needs arguments, you add another level:

```python
def retry(max_attempts=3, exceptions=(Exception,)):
    """
    Decorator factory — returns a decorator.
    Usage: @retry(max_attempts=5)
    """
    def decorator(fn):
        @functools.wraps(fn)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            for attempt in range(max_attempts):
                try:
                    return fn(*args, **kwargs)
                except exceptions as e:
                    if attempt == max_attempts - 1:
                        raise
                    print(f"Attempt {attempt+1} failed: {e}. Retrying...")
        return wrapper
    return decorator

@retry(max_attempts=3, exceptions=(ConnectionError, TimeoutError))
def download_dataset(url):
    # Might fail due to network issues
    ...
```

`@retry(max_attempts=3)` calls `retry(max_attempts=3)`, which returns `decorator`. Then `decorator(download_dataset)` is called, which returns `wrapper`. So `download_dataset` is replaced by `wrapper`, which retries on failure.

Three levels of nesting — `retry` → `decorator` → `wrapper` — is the standard pattern for parameterized decorators. Once you see the pattern you recognize it immediately in real codebases.

---

## Area 4 — Iterators and Generators

### The Iterator Protocol

An iterator is any object that implements two methods: `__iter__` (returns self) and `__next__` (returns the next value, raises StopIteration when done).

```python
class BatchIterator:
    """
    Iterates over a dataset in batches.
    Crucial for training — you never load the full dataset at once.
    """
    def __init__(self, X, y, batch_size, shuffle=True):
        self.X          = X
        self.y          = y
        self.batch_size = batch_size
        self.shuffle    = shuffle

    def __iter__(self):
        n   = len(self.X)
        idx = np.random.permutation(n) if self.shuffle \
              else np.arange(n)

        for start in range(0, n, self.batch_size):
            batch_idx = idx[start : start + self.batch_size]
            yield self.X[batch_idx], self.y[batch_idx]
```

Wait — this uses `yield`, not `return`. That turns the method into a generator. Let me explain generators first, then come back.

---

### Generators — The Most Useful Python Feature You Probably Don't Know

A generator is a function that uses `yield` instead of `return`. When called, it does not execute immediately — it returns a generator object. Each time you call `next()` on the generator, it runs until the next `yield`, produces that value, and then **pauses**. The next call to `next()` resumes from exactly where it paused.

```python
def count_up(start, end):
    """Generator that yields numbers from start to end"""
    current = start
    while current <= end:
        yield current       # pause here, return current
        current += 1        # resume here on next call to next()

gen = count_up(1, 5)
print(next(gen))   # 1 — runs until first yield
print(next(gen))   # 2 — resumes, runs until second yield
print(next(gen))   # 3
# for loop calls next() automatically
for value in count_up(1, 5):
    print(value)   # 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
```

The profound thing about generators is memory. A regular list of a million numbers stores all million numbers in memory simultaneously. A generator produces them one at a time — only one number is in memory at any moment.

For ML, this is critical. Imagine a dataset of 10 million images. Loading all of them into memory is impossible. A generator loads one batch, processes it, discards it, loads the next batch. You can train on datasets far larger than your RAM.

---

### The Dataset and DataLoader Pattern

This pattern appears in every serious ML framework:

```python
class Dataset:
    """Knows how to access individual samples"""
    def __init__(self, X, y):
        self.X = X
        self.y = y

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.X)

    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        return self.X[idx], self.y[idx]


class DataLoader:
    """
    Knows how to batch samples from a Dataset.
    This is exactly what torch.utils.data.DataLoader does.
    """
    def __init__(self, dataset, batch_size=32, shuffle=True):
        self.dataset    = dataset
        self.batch_size = batch_size
        self.shuffle    = shuffle

    def __iter__(self):
        n   = len(self.dataset)
        idx = (np.random.permutation(n) if self.shuffle
               else np.arange(n))

        for start in range(0, n, self.batch_size):
            batch_idx = idx[start : start + self.batch_size]
            # Collect individual samples and stack them
            Xs, ys = zip(*[self.dataset[i] for i in batch_idx])
            yield np.stack(Xs), np.array(ys)

    def __len__(self):
        """Number of batches"""
        return (len(self.dataset) + self.batch_size - 1) // self.batch_size
```

The training loop using this:

```python
dataset    = Dataset(X_train, y_train)
dataloader = DataLoader(dataset, batch_size=32, shuffle=True)

for epoch in range(100):
    for X_batch, y_batch in dataloader:   # __iter__ called here
        yhat = model(X_batch)
        loss = compute_loss(y_batch, yhat)
        model.backward(y_batch, yhat)
        optimizer.update(model.layers)
```

`for X_batch, y_batch in dataloader` calls `dataloader.__iter__()`, which is a generator. Python calls `next()` on it for each iteration. Each call runs the generator until the next `yield`, producing one batch. Between batches, only one batch is in memory.

PyTorch's `DataLoader` is this exact pattern, extended with multiprocessing, prefetching, and collation functions — but the core idea is identical.

---

### Generator Expressions — Concise Generators

Generator expressions are the generator equivalent of list comprehensions:

```python
# List comprehension — stores all values in memory
squares_list = [x**2 for x in range(1_000_000)]

# Generator expression — computes one at a time
squares_gen  = (x**2 for x in range(1_000_000))

# Useful for computing things on the fly
total_params = sum(layer.W.size + layer.b.size
                   for layer in model.layers)

# Works with any function that accepts an iterable
max_weight = max(np.abs(layer.W).max()
                 for layer in model.layers)
```

The parentheses create a generator. No memory for intermediate values. The sum and max functions consume the generator one value at a time.

---

## Area 5 — Context Managers

You saw the dunder method version (`__enter__` and `__exit__`). Python provides a simpler way using `contextlib.contextmanager`:

```python
from contextlib import contextmanager

@contextmanager
def training_mode(model):
    """
    Temporarily set model to training mode, restore after.
    This is exactly what model.train() / model.eval() does in PyTorch.
    """
    original_mode = model.training
    model.training = True
    try:
        yield model          # code inside the with block runs here
    finally:
        model.training = original_mode   # always restores, even on exception

@contextmanager
def no_grad():
    """Disable gradient computation (like torch.no_grad())"""
    original = Layer.compute_grad
    Layer.compute_grad = False
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        Layer.compute_grad = original
```

The `@contextmanager` decorator turns a generator function into a context manager. Everything before `yield` is `__enter__`. The `yield` value is bound to the `as` variable. Everything after `yield` (in the `finally` block) is `__exit__`.

The `finally` block is critical — it runs regardless of whether an exception occurred inside the `with` block. This guarantees cleanup even when things go wrong. Without `finally`, if an exception occurred, the model might be left in training mode permanently.

Usage:

```python
with training_mode(model) as m:
    for X_batch, y_batch in train_loader:
        loss = compute_loss(m, X_batch, y_batch)

# Model automatically restored to original mode here
```

---

## Area 6 — Error Handling Done Properly

Most beginners use `try/except` to silently swallow errors. Proper error handling makes your code fail loudly and informatively when something goes wrong, while recovering gracefully from expected failures.

---

### Custom Exceptions

```python
class ShapeError(ValueError):
    """Raised when tensor shapes are incompatible"""
    pass

class NaNError(RuntimeError):
    """Raised when loss becomes NaN during training"""
    pass

class Layer:
    def forward(self, X):
        if X.shape[1] != self.n_in:
            raise ShapeError(
                f"Layer expects input with {self.n_in} features, "
                f"got {X.shape[1]}. "
                f"Check your architecture — previous layer output "
                f"should match this layer's n_in."
            )
        ...
```

Custom exceptions let callers catch specific error types:

```python
try:
    output = model.forward(X)
except ShapeError as e:
    print(f"Architecture problem: {e}")
    # Handle specifically
except NaNError as e:
    print(f"Training diverged: {e}")
    # Load checkpoint, reduce learning rate
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Unexpected error: {e}")
    raise    # Re-raise if you cannot handle it
```

The `raise` at the end re-raises the exception after printing it. This is the right behavior when you catch something unexpected — log it, but let it propagate. Silently swallowing unexpected exceptions hides bugs.

---

### The EAFP Principle

Python favors "Easier to Ask Forgiveness than Permission" (EAFP) over "Look Before You Leap" (LBYL).

LBYL (not Pythonic):

```python
# Check before trying
if 'weights' in checkpoint:
    if isinstance(checkpoint['weights'], dict):
        if len(checkpoint['weights']) == len(model.layers):
            model.load(checkpoint['weights'])
```

EAFP (Pythonic):

```python
# Try it, handle failure
try:
    model.load(checkpoint['weights'])
except KeyError:
    print("Checkpoint missing 'weights' key")
except TypeError:
    print("Weights in wrong format")
except ValueError as e:
    print(f"Cannot load weights: {e}")
```

The EAFP version is shorter, clearer, and handles race conditions correctly (a key might be deleted between the check and the access in a multithreaded program). It is also more honest — you specify exactly which failures you can handle, rather than trying to predict all possible failure modes upfront.

---

## Putting It All Together — What This Looks Like in Practice

When you read PyTorch, HuggingFace, or any serious ML library, you are reading all six of these concepts simultaneously. A `nn.Module` is:

- A class (OOP)
- With `__call__`, `__repr__`, `__iter__` implemented (Python data model)
- That uses `@property` for parameters (properties)
- Whose training loop uses generators (iterators/generators)
- With `torch.no_grad()` as a context manager
- And custom exceptions like `RuntimeError` with clear messages (error handling)

You now know what every one of those pieces is, why it exists, and how it works.

## Quick Correction — Independence

Your answer for Question 3 was:

> "knowing B does not affect A. the answer will be sum of P(A) and P(B)."

The first part is exactly right. Knowing B tells you nothing new about A. That is the correct intuition.

The second part is wrong, and it is a common confusion. Independence is not about addition. It is about multiplication.

When A and B are independent:

```
P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B)
```

The probability that both happen equals the product of their individual probabilities.

Here is why this makes sense with a concrete example.

You flip two separate coins. What is the probability both land heads?

```
P(first heads)  = 1/2
P(second heads) = 1/2
P(both heads)   = 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/4
```

There are four equally likely outcomes — HH, HT, TH, TT. Only one is both heads. So 1/4. The multiplication gives you the right answer because the coins do not affect each other.

The sum P(A) + P(B) would give 1/2 + 1/2 = 1. That is the probability of A OR B — which is a completely different question. Addition is for union. Multiplication is for independence.

Hold this distinction clearly:

```
P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A ∩ B)     ← union (OR)
P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B)                 ← independence (AND, when independent)
```

This is the foundation that makes everything else in Python make sense. Without it, bugs appear that you cannot explain and behaviour surprises you constantly.
